arXiv:2609.07395v1 [cs.IR] 7 Sep 2026

Uncertainty Quantification for LLM Agents: A
Taxonomy, an Evaluation Protocol, and an Empirical
Study
Moule Lin1 , Qizhen Lan2 , Shuhao Guan3 , Weipeng Jing4 , Jiexin Fan1 , David Gregg1 , Goetz
Botterweck1,*
1

Trinity College Dublin, Ireland
3

2

University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston, USA
4

University College Dublin, Ireland

Northeast Forestry University, China

moulel@tcd.ie, Qizhen.Lan@uth.tmc.edu, shuhao.guan@ucdconnect.ie, jwp@nefu.edu.cn,
jifan@tcd.ie, david.gregg@cs.tcd.ie, goetz.botterweck@tcd.ie

Abstract:
Large language models (LLMs) are no longer deployed only for single-turn conversation but increasingly
act as agents that plan, call tools, retrieve evidence, maintain memory, and interact over long horizons,
often together with other agents through multi-turn conversations. Therefore, knowing when to trust the
agentic system is a prerequisite for safe deployment. However, existing work on quantifying uncertainty
for LLMs was built almost entirely for single-turn question answering. This paper argues that errors and
uncertainty arise from multi-turn conversations, environments, and tools rather than from a single-turn
question answering setting. It comes late, however, and is compounded in a single score that is too coarse to
represent the unreliability. We organize the literature with a three-axis taxonomy, (1) what the uncertainty
is, (2) how it is estimated, and (3) where uncertainty arises during an agent pipeline. We investigate steplevel and trajectory-level calibration and show with a simple counterexample that the first does not imply
the second. Experiments on real agent traces across four models and up to a 50-step budget show that the
proposed metric and reporting protocol (Trajectory-Checkpoint Expected Calibration Error, TC-ECE) can
be computed and that step errors are coupled along a trajectory. We find that confidence estimates from
the agent’s own responses do not consistently outperform a simple baseline. The experiments also show
that averaging all trajectories together can hide overconfidence at later stages, which becomes visible when
results are analyzed across different horizons. In simpler terms, this paper identifies where the uncertainty
comes from in the agentic system pipeline, how to teach agents to know when they are wrong, and why one
confidence number is not enough.
Keywords: Uncertainty Quantification, LLM Agents, Calibration, Hallucination Detection, Tool Use,
Retrieval-Augmented Generation, Multi-Agent Systems
Date: August 2026
Corresponding author (*): Goetz Botterweck, goetz.botterweck@tcd.ie
This work was funded by the European Union’s Horizon Europe 2021–2027 Framework Programme under the Marie SkłodowskaCurie grant agreement No. 101072456. Views and opinions expressed are those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily
reflect those of the European Union or the granting authority.

Use of Large Language Models

1

Introduction
“Doubt is not a pleasant condition, but certainty is an absurd one.”
Voltaire, 1770

The deployment and evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) have changed dramatically. An LLM
was once deployed as a single-turn question-answering system where a question went in and an answer
came out (Brown et al., 2020; Ouyang et al., 2022). Today, however, the same model increasingly performs
as an agent with reasoning capabilities, a system that decomposes goals into plans, invokes external tools
and APIs, retrieves documents, maintains and updates memory, and executes multi-step trajectories in an
environment, frequently communicating with other agents (Yao et al., 2023b; Wang et al., 2024a; Xi et al.,
2025; Yu et al., 2025). This shift undoubtedly improves the capabilities of LLMs but also varies the ways in
which they can fail. A wrong action or erroneous information arising at an early stage will accumulate and
flow in a trajectory, making it almost impossible to retrospectively locate the error points when the final
output is only a sequence of tokens. The main issue is not that agents make mistakes; all statistical methods
make mistakes, but these mistakes occur early, confidently, and irreversibly, in ways that a system-level
checker cannot catch in time. This naturally raises the question: when should we trust the system,
and when should it defer, re-plan, ask, or stop?
Uncertainty is an effective signal for addressing this question. It captures not only uncertainty in the final
answer, but also when, where, and to what extent the model becomes uncertain throughout the trajectory.
There is much research on uncertainty mechanisms in LLMs. However, most existing work still focuses
on single-turn QA, even as agents are increasingly deployed for complex tasks (Oh et al., 2026b). A large
body of work in classical machine learning has studied calibration and uncertainty estimation. This includes
post-hoc recalibration methods (Platt, 1999; Zadrozny & Elkan, 2002), calibration metrics (Naeini et al.,
2015), Bayesian and ensemble-based uncertainty estimation (Gal & Ghahramani, 2016; Blundell et al., 2015;
Lakshminarayanan et al., 2017), methods for separating aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (Kendall & Gal,
2017; Hüllermeier & Waegeman, 2021), and approaches to selective prediction (Geifman & El-Yaniv, 2017;
Kamath et al., 2020). Prior work studies uncertainty in single-turn LLMs through confidence estimation,
calibration, and selective prediction (Brier, 1950; Chow, 1970; Guo et al., 2017; Hüllermeier & Waegeman,
2021). Models can verbalize their output uncertainty by being asked to explicitly report it (Lin et al., 2022a;
Tian et al., 2023; Xiong et al., 2023). Uncertainty can be measured over the space of meanings rather
than surface sequence (Kuhn et al., 2023; Farquhar et al., 2024). Model consistency can be used to detect
hallucinations without external resources (Manakul et al., 2023; Lin et al., 2023). Conformal prediction
brings distribution-free guarantees (Vovk et al., 2005; Quach et al., 2024). These methods are mature, well
benchmarked (Vashurin et al., 2025a; Fadeeva et al., 2023), and increasingly well understood, including their
failure modes (Santilli et al., 2025). However, as agents are given more complex tasks, the object of study is
moving from a single answer to an agentic system (Kirchhof et al., 2025b; Xia et al., 2026a).
Uncertainty in an agentic system is more complex because uncertainty arising at an early stage can accumulate and propagate across subsequent steps (Zhang et al., 2026h; Zhao et al., 2025; Donaldson et al.,
2026). Since each step conditions on the agent’s own previous outputs, errors across steps are correlated. As
Section 3.3 formalizes, per-step calibration therefore provides limited information about the probability that
the entire trajectory succeeds. Uncertainty stems not only from the model’s parameters but from noisy tools
and dynamic environments (Han et al., 2024; Xuan et al., 2026), an external component that no amount of
introspection on the model can estimate. And a final-step confidence score is diagnostically too late. Writing
ht for the interaction history after step t, what the next step needs is a reliability estimate R̂(ht ) available
during the trajectory. The key question is not how likely the trajectory is to fail but whether a wrong
behavior now would change the outcome, which we will formalize as the intervention advantage Ai (ht ) of
Eq. (6) (Zhang et al., 2026c). This paper reviews and organizes the fast-growing but scattered literature on
uncertainty estimation for LLM agents. Our central object is a simple transition from single-turn confidence
to trajectory-level reliability, and this transition needs new methods, new benchmarks, and new conceptual
distinctions. More importantly, the analysis must change from the final output to the trajectory, and the
evaluation standard must change from a correlational perspective to a decision-relevant one. Each of these

Figure 1: Three-axis taxonomy of agent uncertainty: source (A), method family (B), and pipeline stage (C).
Each leaf shows its corpus count and two representative works. Papers may carry multiple tags within each
panel.
changes is already visible in the literature we survey, but no survey has synthesized this research as a scientific
report.
• A documented corpus and a three-axis taxonomy (Figure 1). The taxonomy sorts methods by
source (what the uncertainty is), method family (how it is estimated), and pipeline stage (where it applies).
The axes are largely non-nested. Table 17 in Appendix B classifies every paper, and we release the corpus
with its protocol so the counts can be recomputed (Appendix A).
• A formal lens (Section 3.3). We define step-level and trajectory-level calibration and record the overlooked fact that, under the locally defined step labels the field uses, the first does not compose into the
second. Proposition 2 bounds the error of the marginal-product approximation by measurable error correlations, and intervention advantage states the decision-theoretic target that separates estimation from
control. The lens locates where trajectory calibration breaks rather than repairing it.
• A stage-by-stage methods review (Sections 5 through 8) covering single-turn foundations, tool use
and planning, retrieval and memory, propagation and trajectory-level estimation, multi-agent coordination, abstention and control, and uncertainty-aware training, tracing each stage back to its single-turn
ancestry.
• A trajectory-level metric and a measurement on real traces (Sections 9.2 and 9.3). TC-ECE
(Definition 3) reads calibration at checkpoints along a trajectory rather than at the final answer alone. We
compute it on four models over three experiments at horizons of up to 50 steps. In these configurations
the agent’s stated confidence does not consistently beat a step-index baseline, and pooling checkpoints
hides overconfidence that appears only late. Traces, code, and analysis are released.
• A concrete research agenda (Section 11) distilled from the gaps the taxonomy makes visible, stated
as ten problems, each with the reason it has resisted current tools and the form progress would take
(Table 12).
5

Considering the different needs of readers interested in uncertainty estimation for LLM agents, we provide
several entry points into this paper. Section 4.2 maps the whole structure and marks the section that covers
each block, so a reader can locate any of the entry points below before committing to a path through it. A
practitioner seeking a rapid deployment of an agent can refer directly to the decision guide in Figure 6 and
the method families it points to. A researcher looking for a research problem can start from Section 3.3, and
land on the agenda of Section 11 (Table 12); the heatmap of Figure 3 compresses the same gap analysis into
one view. Newcomers can start with the background and single-turn foundations. These sections introduce
the main concepts used later on, before moving to uncertainty in agentic systems.
We built a documented, multi-source corpus by searching arXiv, the ACM Digital Library, OpenReview,
IEEE Xplore, and PMLR with keywords derived from the taxonomy axes above. Every retained paper was
read and classified independently by two annotators, with an LLM judge proposing a resolution for each
disagreement and a third annotator making the final call; every taxonomy assignment was independently
checked by a second annotator. The resulting core contains 120 papers spanning single-turn foundations,
agentic methods, and related surveys. We also include 454 additional works from the classical UQ, LLM
confidence, agent systems, decision-theoretic, and retrieval literatures that the core builds on; Appendix A
documents the protocol. Figure 2 shows the temporal distribution of the corpus: agent-focused work is nearly
absent before 2024, emerges as a distinct wave through 2025, and grows rapidly into 2026, while single-turn
foundations accumulate steadily throughout. The pattern is worth registering. The field we survey is young
enough that its conventions, its metrics, and its benchmark culture are all still fluid, which is why a synthesis
now can shape them.
A reader who knows the survey literature will ask what this work adds to it. Recent survey works define the
problem and list its challenges. None of them sorts the estimation methods by pipeline stage, and none of
them starts from a set of papers collected in a documented way (Oh et al., 2026b; Kirchhof et al., 2025b; Xia
et al., 2026a). Oh et al. (2026b) do not treat abstention, deferral, or uncertainty-aware training as categories
in their taxonomy. This paper covers all three, and Section 2 sets out the full comparison row by row in
Table 1.

2

Related Work and Positioning

Existing surveys on uncertainty estimation mainly focus on non-agent or single-turn settings, or study agents
without making uncertainty the central object. A pipeline-wide synthesis is therefore needed to organize this
fragmented literature. Building upon previous surveys, we organize the area along three axes: (1) what the
uncertainty is, (2) how it is estimated and used, and (3) where in the agent pipeline the methods operate.
There are some related surveys on uncertainty quantification in LLMs and agents, but none of them covers the
full pipeline that uncertainty comes from. Table 1 summarizes the landscape and compares related surveys
with ours. The comparison shows why the research gap remains open. The first block is uncertainty without
agents, mainly focusing on methods developed for traditional deep learning (Abdar et al., 2021; Gawlikowski
et al., 2023; He et al., 2026a; Wang, 2023) such as Bayesian approximations, ensembles, and single-forwardpass estimators. In these studies, information theory is often used to decompose the uncertainty into an
epistemic part, reducible by adding more training data, and an aleatoric part, generally irreducible regardless
of the amount of data. However, these methods often assume that the data is i.i.d., which does not generally
hold for agents that must interact with an environment over multiple steps.
With the rapid development of LLMs in recent years, a growing body of research moves UQ to single-turn
LLMs (Geng et al., 2024; Huang et al., 2024a; Shorinwa et al., 2025; Xia et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025b).
For example, verbalized uncertainty asks the LLM to explicitly report an uncertainty value for its output;
sampling and consistency methods generate multiple answers and measure the agreement among them.
However, these remain single-generation settings: trajectories, tool calls, and interaction with environments
are not yet considered. Conformal prediction (Campos et al., 2024; Wen et al., 2025) is one branch of
uncertainty quantification. It provides a prediction set with a statistical coverage guarantee for each output.

Figure 2: Quarterly distribution of the 120-paper corpus, pre-2024 pooled. Agent-focused work emerges as
a distinct wave after 2024 and grows rapidly into 2026. The final quarter is incomplete.
The second block focuses on hallucination. These surveys (Ji et al., 2023; Zhang et al., 2023b; Huang et al.,
2025b) mainly study what goes wrong and summarize benchmarks for measuring these failures. However,
they do not treat uncertainty as the main tool for predicting when such failures may occur.
Surveys on the agent side focus more on trustworthiness and security (Yu et al., 2025; Su et al., 2025; He
et al., 2025a), evaluation (Mohammadi et al., 2025), provenance and traceability (Wang et al., 2026g), agent
architectures (Wang et al., 2024a; Xi et al., 2025), multi-agent systems (Guo et al., 2024), memory (Zhang
et al., 2025d), tool learning and augmented language models (Qin et al., 2024a; Mialon et al., 2023), and
communication (Chen et al., 2026a; Sarkar & Sarkar, 2025). In these works, uncertainty appears only as
a paragraph in a trustworthiness taxonomy or a row in an evaluation checklist, rather than as the central
organizing subject. These literatures are broad and sophisticated, but remain largely disconnected on the
questions this paper takes as primary. In particular, they do not provide a unified uncertainty-estimation
framework organized around the full agent pipeline. The closest work is Oh et al. (2026b). They give the
first general formulation of agent UQ, lay out four agent-specific technical challenges, and analyze them
numerically on τ 2 -bench. They also separate the uncertainty of the agent’s own actions from the uncertainty
of what tools and users return; our Panel A follows that split, and our Panel B widens the three estimator
families they compare into eight indexed families. What they do not do is sort methods by pipeline stage,
which is our Panel C. Table 2 makes the relation explicit dimension by dimension.
Three of the contributions listed above have no counterpart there. Section 3.3 records the elementary fact
that step-level calibration does not compose into trajectory-level calibration and quantifies the resulting
gap, so the quantity the field reports is not the quantity deployment needs. Section 9.3 measures the steperror correlation that governs the gap. Abstention, deferral, and uncertainty-aware training, not treated as
categories there, each get a section here.
Xia et al. (2026a) is the closest on the propagation axis: it focuses on how uncertainty propagates through
the system once it has been produced, while we focus on how uncertainty is estimated and which stages of
the agent pipeline these estimation methods apply to.
On the estimator side, the closest single method is Holistic Trajectory Calibration (Zhang et al., 2026h), which
produces a calibrated trajectory confidence from process-level features of the whole run and evaluates it across
eight benchmarks. It is an estimator rather than a synthesis, so it does not appear in Table 1; Section 9.2
explains why it and the TC-ECE protocol proposed here are complementary rather than competing.

3

Background and Preliminaries

This section defines the main concepts used throughout this paper. It reviews how calibration is defined and
measured and explains why uncertainty in language should be computed over meanings rather than tokens.
The formal analysis defines agent trajectories and calibration at the step and trajectory levels. It explains

why calibration at individual steps does not necessarily imply calibration over a full trajectory, a concern
that arises in sequential agent behavior. We use τ = (s0 , a1 , o1 , . . . , aT , oT ) to denote an agent trajectory and
ht = (s0 , a1:t , o1:t ) to denote the history after step t. Let Y ∈ {0, 1} indicate trajectory success, defined by
the task rather than by the steps, and let Yt ∈ {0, 1} indicate success at step t. Under
Q an absorbing-failure
convention, in which no step failure can be repaired later, the two are linked by Y = t Yt . We denote step
confidence by ct = c(at , ht−1 ). At the trajectory level, R = Pr(Y = 1) denotes trajectory reliability, R̂(ht )
its history-conditioned estimate, Ai (ht ) the advantage of an intervention, and U the task utility.
3.1

Sources of Uncertainty

A standard decomposition separates two types of uncertainty. Aleatoric uncertainty is irreducible noise or
ambiguity in the task itself. Epistemic uncertainty reflects the model’s lack of knowledge and can, in principle,
be reduced with more data or computation (Kendall & Gal, 2017; Depeweg et al., 2018; Hüllermeier &
Waegeman, 2021). This distinction has a precise information-theoretic form. Let θ denote model parameters
with posterior p(θ | D). For a single model, let p(y | x, θ) be its predictive distribution. The Bayesian
predictive distribution p(y | x) = Eθ [p(y | x, θ)] has the exact decomposition




+
I y; θ | x
(1)
H Eθ [ p(y | x, θ) ] = Eθ H p(y | x, θ)
|
{z
}
|
{z
}
| {z }
total predictive entropy

expected aleatoric entropy

epistemic mutual information

where the expectations are over θ ∼ p(θ | D). The aleatoric term is the posterior average of the task noise
assigned by each model. The epistemic term measures how much the models in the posterior disagree. It is
zero exactly when the predictive distribution does not depend on θ under the posterior (Depeweg et al., 2018;
Hüllermeier & Waegeman, 2021). Classical deep-learning UQ applies this decomposition by approximating
the posterior. Common approaches include variational weight distributions (Blundell et al., 2015), Monte
Carlo dropout (Gal & Ghahramani, 2016), and deep ensembles (Lakshminarayanan et al., 2017). Ovadia
et al. (2019) tested these methods under distribution shift, and Abdar et al. (2021) and Gawlikowski et al.
9

(2023) provide broader surveys. Scaling these posterior approximations to modern networks remains an
active problem. Recent approaches share stochastic weights across a network (Lin et al., 2025) or use
flow-induced process priors to keep the posterior tractable (Lin et al., 2026b).
For LLMs, this decomposition is difficult to estimate because the true posterior is not available. A deployed
LLM is a single checkpoint, and treating it as a degenerate posterior sets the mutual-information term to
zero, assigning all remaining uncertainty to aleatoric noise even when it reflects missing knowledge; the
decomposition also shifts with the chosen model class and reference distribution (Hüllermeier & Waegeman,
2021; Baan et al., 2023). Existing work therefore uses proxies, including LoRA-ensembles (Balabanov &
Linander, 2024; Yang et al., 2024a), a posterior over the adapter (Lin et al., 2026a), calibration-tuned models
(Kapoor et al., 2024), Bayesian views of in-context learning (Ling et al., 2024), and information-theoretic
bounds from iterative prompting (Yadkori et al., 2024a). The distinction remains observable in practice,
since small probes can separate inherently unanswerable questions from those a larger model can answer, so
hidden states carry information the output distribution alone does not reveal (Ahdritz et al., 2024).
The distinction matters for agents because the two types call for different actions: ambiguity in the request
motivates clarification (Kuhn et al., 2022; Hou et al., 2024), whereas epistemic uncertainty about a fact
motivates retrieval or deferral (Mallen et al., 2023; Shin et al., 2026). Methods that compress both into
one scalar cannot by themselves identify the right response; Yadkori et al. (2024a) isolate the epistemic
component information-theoretically, Hou et al. (2024) attribute disagreement across clarified interpretations
to ambiguity rather than ignorance, and Kirchhof et al. (2025b) argue that even two scalars may not suffice
for agents. Papamarkou et al. (2026) go further and argue that the agent pipeline should itself be Bayesconsistent, with each component reporting a posterior that the orchestration layer combines coherently
instead of thresholding scalars with different meanings. Under this view every Panel B family would output
a distribution and the Panel A labels would become part of the interface between stages. We treat this as a
position rather than an established result, because no agent system in our corpus implements the design.
Agents introduce at least three further sources of uncertainty that are not fully captured by the classical
distinction and do not fit cleanly into the decomposition above. Tools and environments return noisy, partial,
or stale observations (Han et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2024b; Xuan et al., 2026). At step t, this noise is aleatoric
from the agent’s current perspective because it comes from the observation rather than from uncertainty
about the model. The agent can sometimes reduce this uncertainty through another action, such as repeating
a query to an unreliable API or checking a conflicting search result against another source. This possibility
makes the boundary between aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty less clear. Uncertainty at each step can also
accumulate along a trajectory (Zhao et al., 2025; Duan et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2026h). Section 3.3 shows
that this accumulation depends on correlations between errors, which marginal per-step quantities cannot
capture. Communication also passes uncertainty between agents in multi-agent systems (Yoffe et al., 2025;
Huang et al., 2026; Chen et al., 2026b). An agent that accepts a confident claim from another agent inherits
the uncertainty behind that claim, often without knowing its source. Agents may share training histories,
so their agreement may reflect correlated errors rather than independent support. Section 7.3 explains when
this false consensus occurs and how it can be detected. Table 3 summarizes the five sources in Panel A of
our taxonomy and lists representative responses to each source.

3.2

Calibration and Its Metrics

A confidence estimate is calibrated if, among all predictions made with confidence p, a fraction p are correct:
writing Y ∈ {0, 1} for correctness and c for the reported confidence, Pr(Y = 1 | c = p) = p for every p
in the range of c. Calibration does not require confidence to be informative, since the constant predictor
c ≡ Pr(Y = 1) is perfectly calibrated yet says nothing about which predictions are more likely correct. The
concept predates deep learning; the definition matches Dawid’s well-calibrated forecaster (Brier, 1950; Dawid,
1982; Gneiting & Raftery, 2007), and DeGroot & Fienberg (1983) separate calibration from refinement,
the same distinction as reliability against resolution, which explains how a near-constant predictor can
be calibrated but useless. Section 5.4 covers the separate family of methods that correct a miscalibrated
estimate.
10

A3 Tool /

environment

Calibration is typically measured with the expected calibration error (ECE), whose population form is
Ec [ |Pr(Y = 1 | c) − c| ] and whose standard estimator bins n predictions into M confidence bins and
averages the absolute accuracy–confidence gap per bin, weighted by bin massP(Naeini et al., 2015). Proper
scoring rules assess probabilities without binning. The Brier score BS = n1 i (ci − Yi )2 is strictly proper
(Brier, 1950; Gneiting & Raftery, 2007), and Murphy (1973) decompose it into a reliability term that vanishes
under perfect calibration, a resolution term that rewards separating easy from hard cases, and an irreducible
base-rate term, which quantifies the caveat above: a useful signal needs both reliability and resolution. The
logarithmic score is also strictly proper and is the standard sequence-level score for language models (Jiang
et al., 2021; Malinin & Gales, 2021).
Two other families treat confidence as a ranking. AUROC measures how well confidence separates correct
from incorrect outputs, equal (absent ties) to Pr(ci > cj | Yi = 1, Yj = 0), and has been standard for misclassification and hallucination detection since Hendrycks & Gimpel (2017). Selective prediction thresholds
the ranking, answering when c ≥ γ; varying γ trades coverage against selective risk along the risk–coverage
curve, whose area is the AURC (Geifman & El-Yaniv, 2017; El-Yaniv & Wiener, 2010). This follows Chow’s
rejection rule under symmetric costs (Chow, 1970) and extends to learning to defer, where the downstream
expert’s error rate enters the objective (Madras et al., 2018; Mozannar & Sontag, 2020); selective prediction
for LLMs uses the same framework (Cole et al., 2023; Srinivasan et al., 2024), and Section 8.2 gives the
formal statement and its limits for agents. Table 4 lists the metrics used throughout this paper and their
main limitations for agents.
Calibration has important limitations. Kalai & Vempala (2024) show that a language model calibrated
on a corpus must have a nonzero hallucination rate for facts that appear only once in training; this rate
is bounded below by the fraction of such facts. This lower bound implies that calibration alone cannot
eliminate fabrication for arbitrary, low-frequency claims. The result does not imply that calibration is an
unsuitable objective: a miscalibrated model may also hallucinate without providing a reliable signal of
when this occurs. For agents, the practical value of confidence depends on how it guides action. Retrieval,
abstention, or clarification at a checkpoint can prevent errors that confidence estimation alone cannot.
Evidence on LLM calibration is mixed. Kadavath et al. (2022) find that sufficiently large models are approximately calibrated on multiple-choice questions under suitable prompting. They also find that these models
can assess the validity of their own claims, providing early evidence for the self-evaluation signals used by
later methods. Verbalized confidence provides a direct way to elicit such signals. The same study finds
that RLHF-style tuning can make these reports less reliable and more overconfident. Section 5 discusses
elicitation methods, training objectives, and countermeasures in detail.
11

Measurement choices also affect the interpretation of empirical results. The binned ECE is a plug-in estimator
carrying discretization error and finite-sample bias; its value depends on the bin count and on equal-width
against equal-mass binning, and these choices can change method rankings (Nixon et al., 2019; Kumar
et al., 2019; Roelofs et al., 2022). Debiasing, adaptive binning, and consistent kernel or Lp estimators each
address part of the problem, but none makes an arbitrary fixed-bin estimate canonical (Widmann et al.,
2019; Popordanoska et al., 2022). Sequence-level scores add a length confound. Longer outputs accumulate
more log-loss, so a measure correlated with output length can appear predictive merely because correctness
is also length-correlated, an issue that confounds common LLM-UQ evaluations (Santilli et al., 2025); length
normalization reduces but does not remove it (Malinin & Gales, 2021). Agents are especially exposed because
trajectory lengths vary widely, and many metrics in Table 4 also require step-level correctness labels that
are often unavailable.
One further caveat is specific to language. Different token sequences express the same meaning, so token-level
entropy can be high where semantic uncertainty is low; “Paris,” “It’s Paris,” and “The capital of France
is Paris” are one meaning. When correctness is defined semantically, uncertainty should be computed over
meanings rather than surface strings (Kuhn et al., 2023; Farquhar et al., 2024), a point already known
from pre-LLM machine translation and structured prediction (Ott et al., 2018; Malinin & Gales, 2021; Baan
et al., 2023). Section 5.2 reviews estimators that apply this idea. It also discusses a central obstacle to
applying them to agents: sentence equivalence concerns meaning, whereas action equivalence also depends
on task-specific consequences.
3.3

Why Step Calibration Does Not Compose

We model an agent as producing the trajectory τ of Section 3, in which s0 is the initial state, at an action
(including a tool call, retrieval, or message), and ot an observation returned by a tool or the environment.
In a single-turn setting, UQ assigns a confidence score c(a, s0 ) to one action given the initial state. Agent
UQ instead considers a history-dependent confidence estimate at each step, c(at | s0 , a1:t−1 , o1:t−1 ). It also
considers trajectory-level reliability R, defined as the probability that the trajectory achieves the goal. The
distinction between these quantities can be expressed through step-level and trajectory-level calibration.
Q
Throughout this subsection Yt is read as the local goal of step t, so the absorbing-failure link Y = t Yt of
Section 3 applies to locally defined step labels; the remark after Proposition 1 returns to that choice.

Definition 2 (Trajectory-level

Remark. The order of the quantifiers matters: calibration is required separately for each t, not after pooling
(r, t) pairs. Miscalibration at late checkpoints cannot be offset by miscalibration in the opposite direction at
early checkpoints. The conditioning population also changes with t: index t is scored on the survivor subset
of trajectories that run for at least t steps, a subset that shrinks and drifts as the horizon grows. Pooled
variants are weaker; Section 9 discusses them together with the estimation protocol.
Both definitions require a well-defined step label Yt . In practice, this requires choosing what counts as
step-level success. Whether step t achieves its local goal may depend on later events in the trajectory. This
creates a labeling problem, which we discuss in Section 6 and revisit in Section 9. The formal statements
below apply to any fixed choice of Yt , but that choice determines the real-world quantity they describe.
Definition 1 captures the calibration target commonly used in single-turn UQ (Kadavath et al., 2022; Guo
et al., 2017), whereas Definition 2 gives the trajectory-level targetQrelevant to agent deployment. Both
propositions below assume the absorbing-failure convention Y = t Yt , and their scope is exactly that
assumption. Agents that retry a failed tool call, re-issue a query, or re-plan after a wrong step do not satisfy
it, and for them a step failure does not determine Y ; Section 8.2 treats recoverability as the property that
separates two histories carrying equal risk. Definition 2, Definition 3, and the experiment of Section 9.3

do not rely on the convention, because they score R̂(ht ) against an independently observed task outcome
Y rather than against a product of step labels. A simple way to relate the two is to multiply confidence
scores across steps, but this calculation generally ignores dependence among step outcomes. For dependent
Bernoulli variables, the product of marginal probabilities need not equal the joint probability. Proposition 1
records this standard result for agent trajectories and separates the two calibration notions used in this paper.
This distinction matters when a running product of step confidences is used as an estimate of trajectory
reliability. Proposition 2 later quantifies the error in the product of step marginals and identifies its source.
Proposition 1 (Calibration does not compose) There areQ
agents whose step-level confidence estimates
are perfectly calibrated according to Definition 1, but the product t ct can still be miscalibrated for trajectorylevel success, either overestimating or underestimating the true probability of success.
Proof sketch. Consider a two-step agent in which both steps are always executed, so that Y = Y1 Y2 . Suppose
the success of both steps depends on the same fair coin. In the correlated case, both steps succeed when
the coin lands heads. Each step succeeds with probability 12 , so the constant estimator ct = 12 is perfectly
calibrated at the step level. Multiplying the two confidence scores gives 41 , even though the true trajectory
13

success probability is 12 . In the anti-correlated case, step 1 succeeds on heads while step 2 succeeds on tails.
The same step-level estimator is still perfectly calibrated, and the product still gives 14 , but now the true
trajectory success probability is 0. □
The discrepancy arises from dependence among step outcomes, which per-step confidence scores alone do
not capture. For two steps, the effect can be written explicitly. For two steps with non-degenerate outcomes,
let p1 = Pr(Y1 = 1) and p2 = Pr(Y2 = 1) be the marginal success probabilities, and let ρ be the Pearson
correlation between Y1 and Y2 . Their joint success probability is
p
p
(2)
Pr(Y1 = 1, Y2 = 1) = p1 p2 + ρ p1 (1 − p1 ) p2 (1 − p2 )
The joint probability varies linearly with ρ, equals the independence product at ρ = 0, and reaches the
Fréchet–Hoeffding bounds max(0, p1 + p2 − 1) ≤ Pr(Y1 = 1, Y2 = 1) ≤ min(p1 , p2 ) at the extremes; the
proof sketch’s two examples are exactly ρ = 1 (joint probability 21 ) and ρ = −1 (joint probability 0).
Here p1 and p2 are step marginals, not the reported confidences of Definition 1, which satisfy E[ct ] = pt
under step-level calibration without any individual ct having to equal pt ; the remark after Proposition 2
develops this distinction. Early errors can affect later decisions and induce positive correlations among
step outcomes (Zhang et al., 2026h; Zhou et al., 2026c), in which case multiplying the step marginals
underestimates joint success. Neither scalar product identifies which step is likely to fail, and beyond two
steps pairwise correlations do not determine the joint distribution, so marginals and pairwise statistics alone
cannot guarantee calibrated trajectory-level confidence. This motivates propagation methods that model
dependence across steps rather than assuming independence (Zhao et al., 2025; Duan et al., 2025), reviewed
in Section 8.1, and explicitly Markovian formulations of trajectory reliability (Tran-Truong & Le, 2026).
Remark (the counterexample depends on the step-label convention). Proposition 1 is a statement about locally
defined step labels rather than about trajectories as such. Consider instead a survival convention thatQsets
Yt = 1 exactly when the prefix ht still admits a successful continuation. Under this convention, Y = t Yt
holds by construction rather than by assumption, and the anti-correlated branch of the counterexample
dissolves. When step 1 achieves its local goal but makes the failure of step 2 inevitable, the survival label sets
Y1 = 0, so the constant estimator c1 = 21 is no longer calibrated for that label. Non-composition is therefore
a product of the local-goal convention, and the survival convention removes it at a price. The survival label
depends on whether any successful continuation exists, which is not locally observable during execution,
and estimating it is precisely the trajectory-reliability problem R̂(ht ) that this paper studies. Choosing a
step-label convention is thus a modeling decision that trades local observability against composability.
The chain rule gives an exact product expression in terms of conditional step-success probabilities. Comparing this expression with the product of marginal step-success probabilities yields a bound on the approximation error. Let λt = Pr (Yt = 0 | Y1:t−1 = 1) denote the conditional probability that step t fails given that
all earlier steps succeeded. By the chain rule,

Whenever the conditional probabilities are defined, Eq. (3) is exact regardless of dependence among step

Proposition 2 bounds the error of the marginal-product approximation t pt . The bound depends on the
step marginals, prefix reliabilities, and correlations between each step outcome and the corresponding cleanprefix indicator. We call these correlations prefix-coupling coefficients. At k = 2 the coefficient coincides
with the pairwise correlation between the first two step outcomes, but at later steps it couples Yk to the
joint indicator of a clean prefix and should not be read as a pairwise correlation between two steps. All
three quantities can be estimated from held-out trajectories to assess the possible size of the approximation
error.

Remark
Q (relation to the reported-confidence product t ct ). The estimator used in practice is t ct (ht−1 ),
not t pt . Step-level calibration links each reported confidence to its marginal success probability only
in expectation. If each ct is calibrated in the sense of Definition 1, then E[ct ] = pt , but a realized value
ct (ht−1 ) need not equal
Q pt , and expectation cannot in general be moved through the product. The observed
composition error t ct − R therefore separates pathwise into a reported-confidence–marginal gap and the
dependence term of Proposition 2, two mechanisms that are often combined under the phrase “the product
rule fails” and that can act in opposite directions. Only the dependence term is controlled here; under
nonnegative clean-prefix correlations it makes the marginal product conservative, so a large overestimate
of trajectory reliability cannot be explained by positive cross-step dependence alone and instead motivates
separate checks of the calibration and dependence structure of the reported confidences ct . Appendix C.2
states the identity,
quantifies both terms on the traces of Section 9.3, and derives a companion bound on the
Q
mean bias of t ct ; neither bound establishes calibration of the product, and a multicalibration-style result
that would is listed as an open problem in Section 11.
Proposition 2 has practical implications. The marginal product may be a useful approximation for short
trajectories and for pipelines that reduce step dependence through independent verification or context isolation, and the bound, whose ingredients ρk , pk , and Rk−1 are all measurable on labeled trajectories, quantifies
how far the product can stray. The proposition also explains why p
long trajectories are difficult to estimate,
a point distinct from the exponential decay of R itself: the factor (1 − Rk−1 )/Rk−1 grows as prefix reliability falls, so even modest coupling opens a large gap at long horizons, and under nonnegative clean-prefix
correlations the marginal product understates reliability. For the same reason the bound degrades with the
horizon; in a representative T = 20 setting it exceeds the trivial bound of 1 once correlations reach roughly
0.13, so it is mainly informative for short horizons or near-zero coupling (Appendix C), although the signed
decomposition of Appendix C.2 remains valid at every horizon. Estimating qk and ρk also requires labeled
trajectories from the deployment distribution, and clean prefixes become rare as the horizon grows, so when
15

enough labels exist, fitting a trajectory-level calibrator directly may be preferable; the proposition explains
how reliability depends on step relationships rather than replacing empirical calibration.
The practical value of the bound therefore depends on the strength of step dependence observed in practice.
Direct measurements in deployed agents remain scarce. Prior work mainly provides qualitative evidence that
early mistakes propagate (Zhang et al., 2026h; Zhou et al., 2026c). Section 9.3 measures this dependence
directly on chained QA items. It finds a small positive step-error correlation in one of four conditions:
reasoning chains that share a context. In that condition, realized reliability differs from the marginal product
in the direction predicted by Proposition 2 and remains within its bound. The empirical evidence is limited:
the interval barely excludes zero, only one of four tested conditions is positive, and no multiplicity correction
is applied. These results support a hypothesis about reused reasoning content rather than an established
mechanism. Section 9.3 also measures the coupling on ALFWorld, where a real action–observation loop and
exact per-step labels are available. There the within-game prefix-coupling coefficient on outcome-tracking
step labels is positive, with game-bootstrap intervals that exclude zero in both models tested, and the
marginal product understates prefix reliability at every horizon beyond a few steps, in the direction and
within the bound that the proposition gives. The coupling is therefore stronger in a genuine agentic loop,
where later steps directly use earlier actions and observations, than in chains that share only a context
window. We did not find direct measurements of this effect elsewhere in our corpus. Equation (3) gives
a survival-based view of trajectory reliability, with λt playing the role of a discrete-time hazard in the
sense of classical survival analysis (Kalbfleisch & Prentice, 2002). At each step, the relevant quantity is
the probability of success conditional on all previous steps having succeeded, rather than the marginal
success probability. For online monitoring, the hazard can also be conditioned on the observed history:
λt (ht−1 ) = Pr (Yt = 0 | Y1:t−1 = 1, ht−1 ). Averaging this quantity over histories that reach step t without
an earlier failure recovers λt . Equation (3) distinguishes the valid multiplication
Q of conditional survival
probabilities from the naive multiplication of marginals. The naive product t ct is not guaranteed to
condition on all previous steps having succeeded, and the equality qt = pt that would license it, with
independence as one sufficient condition, can fail in agent trajectories because earlier errors may remain
hidden in the observable history (Zhang et al., 2026h). The equation also shows the horizon effect directly:
a constant hazard gives R = (1 − λ)T , so reliability decays exponentially with length, which raises the stakes
for long-horizon execution (Wei et al., 2026), and the stepwise hazard λt (ht−1 ) is a natural target for the
monitoring methods of Section 8.2. Hazard estimation also creates a statistical challenge for protocols based
on Definition 2. The hazard at step t is informed only by the roughly N Rt−1 trajectories that survive to it,
an effective sample size that shrinks geometrically
with the horizon and gives the marginal-hazard estimate a

standard error of order O (N Rt−1 )−1/2 ; conditioning on the history requires further modeling assumptions.
Trajectory-calibration results should therefore be reported separately by horizon with group sizes, so that
poor late-step calibration is not hidden under the mass of early-step examples (Section 9), and rollout-based
labeling can supply Monte Carlo estimates of Pr(Y = 1 | ht ) where surviving prefixes are rare (Wang et al.,
2024b).
The chain rule for entropy gives a related but weaker view, separating uncertainty in the agent’s actions from
uncertainty introduced by tools and the environment (Han et al., 2024). Even that full decomposition does
not provide trajectory reliability, because policy entropy may reflect benign diversity among equally valid
actions rather than the probability of task success (Kuhn et al., 2023). Appendix C states the decomposition
and its limits. Trajectory reliability alone does not determine an intervention decision. A calibrated R̂(ht )
estimates the probability of trajectory failure but does not determine whether intervention improves expected
utility. Following Zhang et al. (2026c), we define the intervention advantage of an oversight action i at history
ht as
Ai (ht ) = E [U | do(i), ht ] − E [U | continue, ht ]
(6)
where U is the task utility. If intervention costs are not included in U , an intervention is beneficial when its
expected utility gain exceeds its cost. Under the loss assumptions of Chow’s rule, thresholding a calibrated
risk score is optimal in a one-step setting, whereas learning-to-defer routes selected cases to another expert
(Chow, 1970; Madras et al., 2018; Mozannar & Sontag, 2020). Section 8.2 gives the formal statement and
explains its failure modes for agents. For agent trajectories, the same failure risk does not always imply
the same need for intervention. Two trajectories may have the same value of 1 − R̂(ht ) but differ greatly
in how easy it is to recover from an error. For example, opening an incorrect file may be easy to reverse,
16

whereas sending an email to the wrong recipient may have irreversible consequences. Similar differences
between reversible and irreversible actions also appear in control settings studied by Greenblatt et al. (2024).
Thresholding a calibrated risk score can therefore be insufficient. Risk quantifies the probability of failure,
whereas intervention advantage quantifies whether an action is expected to improve utility. We return to
this distinction in Sections 8.2 and 11.
Appendix D connects these quantities to classical decision theory, including POMDPs and the value of
information.
Takeaway (Section 3.3). Step-level calibration does not compose into trajectory-level calibration. The observed
composition error separates into a step-miscalibration term and a dependence term that can act in opposite directions, and only the dependence term is bounded by Proposition 2. The quantity to estimate online is the
history-conditioned hazard λt (ht−1 ) of Eq. (3), whose effective sample size decays geometrically with the horizon.
Even a calibrated risk estimate does not by itself determine when to intervene; that decision is governed by the
intervention advantage Ai (ht ) of Eq. (6).

4

The Taxonomy and Paper Roadmap

This section defines the three axes used to classify every method in the corpus. It also explains how the
remaining sections use these axes. We also distinguish the interaction regime in which a method operates;
the roadmap in Section 4.2 tags each methods section with the regime it covers. There are four regimes: a
single-turn setting, in which one prompt produces one answer; a one agent, many steps setting, in which one agent
conditions on its earlier outputs across a trajectory; a multi-agent setting, in which several agents exchange
messages; and the whole trajectory view, which treats the full run as one object and studies how uncertainty
accumulates along it. The distinction between one agent, many steps and multi-agent is important. Both are agentic
settings, but they involve different dependence structures. Section 3.3 studies correlation associated with
one agent conditioning on its own earlier outputs. Section 7.3 studies correlation associated with shared
training histories and communication across agents. Combining these settings into one category would hide
which dependence structure a method addresses.
4.1

Three Taxonomic Axes and the Distribution of Existing Work

Our taxonomy describes each method along three axes. Figure 1 presents them as Panel A, Panel B,
and Panel C, in the order used in this paper. Panel A identifies the source of uncertainty addressed by
a method. Table 3 introduces these sources, and Section 3.1 discusses them in detail. Panel B identifies
the method family. It contains nine indexed families, B1–B9. Five are uncertainty estimators inherited
from single-turn research. Three are agent-native families: uncertainty propagation and trajectory-level
estimation, abstention and deferral, and uncertainty-aware training. The remaining other family contains
analyses, benchmarks, and surveys that introduce no method of their own. Panel C identifies the pipeline
stage at which a method is applied. Its categories include planning, tool use, retrieval, memory, multi-step
reasoning, and multi-agent coordination. Single-turn and agent-general methods are recorded as broader
categories.
The panels answer different questions. Panel A describes what a method aims to measure, Panel B describes
how uncertainty is estimated or used, and Panel C describes where the method operates. For most labels,
a paper’s value on one panel does not determine its value on another. The cross-tabulation in Figure 3
therefore captures combinations across axes rather than reducing to a block-diagonal structure. We do not
claim that the axes are statistically independent or that they partition the corpus. Figure 3 shows a nonuniform distribution, and some label pairs are structurally related. A paper may also receive several source,
family, or stage tags. One pair is nested by definition. Inter-agent uncertainty A5 exists only when agents
communicate. Every paper with this source tag therefore also has the multi-agent stage tag C6. The reverse
implication does not hold. Of the 23 corpus papers tagged C6, 12 study uncertainty created or transmitted
through communication and consensus and therefore carry A5. The remaining 11 use a multi-agent scaffold
for another purpose, such as a domain application or an architecture survey, and assign uncertainty to other
sources.
17

The labels within a panel do not always sit at the same level of abstraction. Panel A places the two classical
uncertainty types beside three labels that describe where uncertainty enters or how it moves, and these agentic
labels can overlap the classical pair. Panel B combines estimation mechanisms with a control mechanism,
abstention and deferral B7, and a learning procedure, uncertainty-aware training B8, so it should be read
as describing how uncertainty is estimated or used. Panel C likewise combines pipeline components with an
interaction regime and an architectural setting, which is why the interaction regime is marked separately by
the roadmap badges (Section 4.2) rather than read off Panel C. Appendix E records these conventions in
full. It explains why the five source labels remain one panel, why estimation, control, and training are not
split into a separate response axis, and how a four-dimensional reading of the taxonomy can be recovered
from the three panels.
Figure 1 provides the canonical definition of Panel B. It contains exactly nine indexed families, B1–B9, and
these meanings remain fixed throughout this paper. The other displays are projections of this taxonomy.
Table 5 expands the eight indexed families B1–B8. It omits the residual category B9 and adds two unindexed
practical rows, internal-state probes and post-hoc calibration, for a total of ten rows. Table 17 in Appendix B
retains these labels and adds an unindexed self-evaluation label to make the background literature easier to
locate. These entries are evidence-table subcategories rather than additional Panel-B families. Figure 3 uses
only the eight indexed columns B1–B8. One family has different short labels across displays. Category B8
is called training and distillation in Figure 1, uncertainty-aware training in Table 5, and distillation in the
column header of Figure 3. All three label the same family, which Section 8.3 treats as covering both topics.
The leaf counts in Figure 1 are tag counts rather than paper counts. Papers may carry several tags per
panel, some papers carry no tag on a given panel, and calibration is deliberately recorded as a property
rather than a leaf, as explained below. The counts therefore neither cover every paper on every panel nor
sum to the corpus size. Appendix E gives the exact per-panel accounting. The clearest case is memory,
whose 31 papers mostly co-occur with retrieval, which is why Section 7.2 describes memory-specific methods
as scarce despite the large stage count.
Crossing Panel C with Panel B gives the matrix in Figure 3. We present the figure in Section 6, after
introducing the relevant mechanisms. Its distribution also explains the relative emphasis of later sections.
The largest cell is retrieval × sampling and consistency, with 18 corpus papers. This concentration may
reflect how readily single-turn resampling transfers to retrieval settings, where correctness labels are often
available, horizons are short, and decisions are relatively local. The next largest cell is multi-step reasoning
× propagation, with 15 papers. This combination is more closely associated with agentic settings because
errors can accumulate across long trajectories. Calibration is not a heatmap column. A paper may report
calibration while using any estimation mechanism, so we record calibration as a separate property. Treating it
as a family would make it the largest cell without explaining how the underlying methods operate. Sparse cells
have different causes. Some combinations are structurally difficult. For example, conformal methods in multiagent settings face strong inter-agent dependence and may violate standard exchangeability assumptions.
Other gaps have less direct technical explanations. Verbalized confidence has received limited attention
in planning and multi-agent settings despite its low cost. Distillation methods rarely evaluate whether
trajectory-level calibration transfers to the student, even though process-level supervision could support
such an evaluation. Probe-based methods have also rarely been applied to action correctness, as discussed
in Section 5. These under-covered combinations motivate several directions in Section 11; the taxonomyindependent sensitivity searches in Appendix A identify neighboring work and delimit these claims. Two
limitations apply when interpreting sparse cells. The search terms were derived from these axes, as described
in Appendix A. A cell may therefore appear sparse because another research community uses different
terminology. The search covered arXiv, the ACM Digital Library, OpenReview, IEEE Xplore, and PMLR,
but coverage can still vary across research communities and platform-specific vocabularies. Work in statistics,
robotics, control, human–computer interaction, and decision theory was also examined through referencelist checks and targeted follow-up searches. Section 11 therefore describes sparse cells as under-covered
combinations in the surveyed literature. Where a neighboring field contains relevant work, we identify that
connection rather than infer absence from the corpus count alone.

18

4.2

A Roadmap to the Remaining Sections

This subsection summarizes the structure of this paper and marks the section that covers each block. The
badges below mark the interaction regime each section studies. Section 5 reviews the single-turn inheritance,
and Section 6 examines what changes when the same model instead acts over a trajectory. The two methods
sections organize the same corpus along different axes. Section 7 uses Panel C, the pipeline stage, because its
methods differ mainly in where they operate in the agent loop: its planning, tool-use, retrieval, memory, and
perception-surface stages study a one agent, many steps setting, and its coordination stage the multi-agent setting.
The method families used at each stage are diverse and secondary to the setting. Section 8 uses Panel B,
the method family. Propagation, control, and uncertainty-aware training apply across stages rather than
belonging to one stage: propagation and the evaluation protocol of Section 9 take the whole trajectory view,
abstention and deferral act during a one agent, many steps run, and uncertainty-aware training draws its objectives
from all four regimes. These organizations are complementary. A stage-based section identifies which families
appear at a given stage, while a family-based section identifies the stages to which a family has been applied.
Both sections use Panel A to distinguish the uncertainty source addressed by each method.

4.3

What This Paper Reports and What It Proposes

Most claims in this paper report findings from prior work. Our proposals include the three-panel taxonomy
and its indexing in Figure 1; Propositions 1 and 2, together with Definitions 1 and 2; and a trajectory-aware
reporting protocol, of which the TC-ECE statistic in Definition 3 is one component alongside trajectory-level
resampling, the causally restricted horizon baseline, position stratification, the signed calibration gap, and the
nested bootstrap. They also include the three experiments in Section 9.3, the measured cost–quality tradeoff in Figure 5, and the ten-problem agenda in Section 11, including its interpretation of sparse taxonomy
cells. The claim that oversight should be evaluated through intervention advantage rather than risk is due
to Zhang et al. (2026c), and we adopt it here. All other claims are attributed to their sources. Appendix I
states where large language models were and were not used in preparing this paper. Appendix A documents
the construction of the 120-paper core corpus, the broader set of 454 background works used in the narrative
synthesis, and the targeted gap audit used to examine selected sparse combinations independently of the
taxonomy labels. Works outside the core corpus are marked with ◦ in Table 17. The core corpus provides
the evidentiary basis for corpus-level quantitative claims, including the taxonomy counts, heatmap, and
statements about the distribution of the surveyed literature. The broader narrative synthesis also draws
on background work from uncertainty estimation, statistics, decision theory, control, robotics, information
retrieval, software engineering, and human–computer interaction, particularly when connecting the agentUQ literature to adjacent research areas. These background works provide conceptual and methodological
context but do not enter the 120-paper corpus counts or the taxonomy heatmap. Accordingly, claims about
sparsity or prevalence are stated specifically with respect to the core corpus rather than as claims about the
absence of work from the full literature. For selected sparse combinations that motivate the research agenda,
Appendix A additionally reports a taxonomy-independent targeted search used to identify neighboring work
and to delimit the corresponding claims. We release the complete taxonomy assignment for the core corpus
as a machine-readable table in data/taxonomy_table.csv. Each row records one core-corpus paper and
its pipeline-stage, uncertainty-source, and method-family tags, supporting direct corpus queries. Table 17
is a broader evidence table and reading guide: it contains the core-corpus papers together with selected
background works, and should therefore not be interpreted as a direct printed copy of the machine-readable
core-corpus table.
Takeaway (Section 4). Every corpus paper is indexed along three axes. Panel A records the uncertainty source,
Panel B records how uncertainty is estimated or used, and Panel C records the pipeline stage. The axes ask largely
non-nested questions but are neither statistically independent nor a partition, and leaf counts are tag counts rather
than paper counts (Appendix E). Section 7 walks the corpus by stage and Section 8 by family, while the sparse
cells of Figure 3 motivate the agenda of Section 11.

19

5

Single-Turn LLMs

5.1

Problem Setting

Uncertainty estimation for agents builds on techniques developed for single-turn models. This section reviews
these techniques and their underlying assumptions. Dedicated surveys cover this material in depth (Geng
et al., 2024; Huang et al., 2024a; Shorinwa et al., 2025; Xia et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025b). Our narrower aim is
to summarize the model access required by each family, its computational cost, and the limitations that arise
when the model operates as an agent rather than as a single-turn answer generator. The subsections below
therefore keep the family-level view and the agent-facing limitations; Appendix F reviews the individual
methods within each family. The object of study is a model pθ (y | x) over outputs y given an input x. An
uncertainty estimator maps the input, the generated output ŷ, and the available model information to either
a scalar confidence c(x, ŷ) ∈ [0, 1] or a set-valued prediction. We evaluate an estimator by its calibration, its
ability to rank correct outputs above incorrect outputs, and any formal guarantees it provides (Section 3.2).
Table 5 compares the eight indexed families B1–B8 by their core ideas, model access, computational cost,
and representative works. Five families are inherited from single-turn work, and three are agent-native.
The table also includes two unindexed rows, internal-state probes and post-hoc calibration, for practical
comparison. The ten rows do not correspond to ten taxonomy families. Section 4 defines the nine Panel-B
families, including the residual category B9, which is omitted here.
5.2

Black-Box Signals: Verbalization, Sampling, and Consistency

Two inherited families require only the model’s text output. This limited access requirement makes them
common choices for agents built on closed APIs. Verbalized confidence directly elicits a confidence statement
in natural language, beginning with dialogue agents trained to hedge at rates matching their empirical error
(Mielke et al., 2022) and models fine-tuned to append a stated probability under what amounts to a proper
scoring rule (Lin et al., 2022a). Calibration of the elicited value depends on the elicitation format and the
prompt wording, and alignment tuning can degrade the calibration of the underlying token probabilities
(Tian et al., 2023; Xiong et al., 2023; OpenAI et al., 2023). Appendix F.1 reviews the elicitation formats,
the training-based refinements, and the recent verbalized-confidence literature. Verbalized confidence is
compatible with black-box agent pipelines because a step-level score ct can be elicited alongside each action
at at little additional inference cost. A common limitation is systematic overconfidence. Models tend to
produce high, rounded scores and hedge less than human respondents, while reward-model training may
reinforce this pattern (Zhou et al., 2023b; 2024a; Leng et al., 2024). The effect can become more pronounced
over multi-step trajectories (Xuan et al., 2026). A separate limitation concerns the interpretation of reported
confidence. Turpin et al. (2023) show that chain-of-thought rationales can be systematically unfaithful: when
an unreported feature influences an answer, a model may generate a plausible justification that does not
reflect the computation that produced the answer. Verbalized confidence is produced through a similar
output process. It may therefore be calibrated as a distribution-level statistic without reliably representing
the model’s internal state for a particular input. Sharma et al. (2024) further show that such reports can
adapt to perceived interlocutor preferences. This issue is important for agents because a step-level score
ct is elicited from a context produced partly by the agent and may be used as evidence by another agent
(Section 7.3). Confidence in an action also requires predicting the environment’s response, which may lie
outside the model’s training objective (Section 6).
Another black-box approach samples multiple generations and measures their agreement, as in selfconsistency decoding, where the vote share of the most common answer serves as the confidence score
(Wei et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2023b). Because different token sequences can express the same meaning
(Section 3.2), sampling-based scores are usually computed over meanings rather than surface strings. Semantic entropy clusters the samples into equivalence classes with bidirectional NLI entailment and computes
entropy over the classes (Kuhn et al., 2023; Farquhar et al., 2024), and probes can predict it from a single forward pass (Kossen et al., 2024). Appendix F.1 states the estimator, its refinements, and the wider
consistency-based family (Manakul et al., 2023; Lin et al., 2023). These methods can be applied to agents
by resampling individual tool decisions or complete trajectories, although the cost grows with the horizon
T . Meaning-level uncertainty is harder to apply to actions than to plain text because surface form does not
